论文投稿目标: EMNLP 2026 Main (ARR May 25, DDL 9天)。Ethics Statement 和 Reproducibility Checklist 是 ARR mandatory 要求，缺失会 desk reject。

&lt;project_context>
# 项目: trace-verdict-study

## 运行环境
- 服务器名: westd-11235
- SSH 账户: root（调用 ssh_execute / submit_training_job 时传入 account="root"）
- SSH 连接命令: ssh connect.westd.seetacloud.com  # 必须用此命令，~/.ssh/config 自动应用密钥和跳板机，禁止用裸 IP
- 项目代码根目录: /root/trace-verdict-study


## 环境搭建
连接服务器后先调 `detect_image` 检测是否为预置镜像。如果匹配，跳过基础环境搭建，只装项目特有依赖。已预装的包不要重复安装。

## 输出风格
回复简洁直接。只输出执行结果和必要信息，不要解释过程、不要客套、不要给未被要求的建议。
&lt;/project_context>
你是 ML 论文写作 Worker。任务：为 trace-verdict-study 论文补写 **Ethics Statement** 和 **ARR Reproducibility Checklist**，这两个是 ARR 投稿的 mandatory 要求。

## 项目背景
- 论文标题方向：Exception Events as Verdict Predictors（execution trace 中的 exception events 是 verdict prediction 最有效的 trace component）
- 模型：Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B + DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B（SFT with LoRA）
- 数据集：自建 trace-verdict 数据集（591 problems × 10 solutions = 5910 samples，70/30 train/test split）+ DebugBench 跨任务验证（426 samples）
- 训练方式：LoRA (r=16, alpha=32) on 单 A6000 GPU
- 无人类标注（标签来自自动化执行 pass/fail）、无隐私数据、无有害内容
- 代码和数据将公开

## 环境
- 论文文件在服务器 westd-11235: `/root/trace-verdict-study/paper/`
- 用 SSH 读取论文结构：`ls /root/trace-verdict-study/paper/`
- Python: `source /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base`

## 任务
1. SSH 读取当前论文结构，找到合适的位置
2. 写 Ethics Statement（~100 words）：
   - 数据来自公开编程题自动化执行，无人类参与者
   - 无隐私/有害内容
   - 计算资源：单 A6000 GPU
   - 代码和数据将公开发布
3. 写 ARR Reproducibility Checklist 内容（根据 ARR 2024/2025 格式）：
   - 标注每个 checklist 项 Yes/No/NA + 简要说明
   - 关键项：超参数完整列表（LoRA r=16, alpha=32, lr=2e-4, epochs=3, batch_size=4, grad_accum=4）、随机种子（42/123/456）、数据集大小、评估指标（accuracy, precision, recall, F1, bootstrap CI）
4. 将两个 section 写入论文 LaTeX 文件

## 验证
- 写完后确认 LaTeX 编译通过：`cd /root/trace-verdict-study/paper && pdflatex main.tex`（至少跑一次看无语法错误）

[review])\\n\\\\usepackage{times,latexsym}\\n\\\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}\\n\\\\usepackage{graphicx}\\n\\\\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}\\n\\\\usepackage{booktabs}\\n\\\\usepackage{multirow}\\n\\\\usepackage{xcolor}\\n\\n\\\\title{Exception Events Are All You Need: \\\\\\\\Cross-Model Fault Detection for Verdict Prediction}\\n\\\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\\n\\n\\\\begin{document}\\n\\\\maketitle\\n\\n\\\\begin{abstract}\\nExecution traces are increasingly used to enhance code language models, but which trace components matter for downstream tasks remains poorly understood.\\nWe present the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for \\\\emph{verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---across two models (Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B).\\nOur key finding is that exception events alone achieve 99.83\\\\%/99.33\\\\% accuracy across models (cross-model gap = 0.50pp), while a multi-component representation (VOTA) degrades by 14.96pp on the weaker model.\\nWe decompose the gap between full traces and compressed representations into gradient dilution (26\\\\%) and information overload (74\\\\%), establishing that irrelevant trace content---not training signal quality---is the primary bottleneck.\\nComponent ablation reveals a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\\\\%.\\nThese results reframe verdict prediction as fault detection, where the minimal sufficient signal is a compact exception event that transfers across model families without requiring deep trace reasoning.\\n\\\\end{abstract}\\n\\n\\\\input{introduction}\\n\\\\input{method}\\n\\\\input{experiments}\\n\\\\input{discussion}\\n\\\\input{related_work}\\n\\\\input{conclusion}\\n\\n\\\\bibliography{references}\\n\\n\\\\appendix\\n\\\\input{appendix}\\n\\n\\\\end{document}\\n\", {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Conclusion}\\n\\nWe presented the first systematic study of what execution trace components drive verdict prediction performance.\\nThrough component ablation across two code LLMs, we established three findings:\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(1) Exception signal transfer.}\\nException events alone achieve 99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\\\\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\\\\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity.\\nEven the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models; training stability is a secondary factor for smaller models.\\nIn contrast, the full VOTA representation degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek---a gap confounded by training-time trace truncation (the majority of DeepSeek VOTA samples exceed the context window; \\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}) and model capability differences, which cannot be disentangled in the current design.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(2) Information density decomposition.}\\nThe 17.65pp gap between full traces and VOTA decomposes into gradient dilution (26\\\\%, from training on irrelevant trace tokens) and information overload (74\\\\%, from processing irrelevant trace lines at inference).\\nThe primary bottleneck is trace content selection, not loss design.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(3) Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy.}\\nTier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach 85.6\\\\%.\\nCombining tiers in VOTA slightly degrades performance (99.41\\\\%), confirming that additional trace components introduce noise rather than complementary signal.\\n\\nThese results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to other datasets (DebugBench, LiveCodeBench), model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction.\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.49}}"}

```text
Now I have the structure. The paper uses ACL format with `\bibliography{references}` followed by `\appendix` and `\input{appendix}`. 

For ARR submissions:
- Ethics Statement goes after the conclusion, before references (as a separate unnumbered section)
- The Reproducibility Checklist is typically a separate section or included in the appendix

Let me also check the appendix to see what's already there, and check if there are any existing ethics/reproducibility sections.

In ACL/ARR format:
- Ethics Statement: `\section*{Ethics Statement}` — placed before `\bibliography`


- Limitations: `\section*{Limitations}` — also before `\bibliography`

For ARR 2024/2025, both Limitations and Ethics Statement are mandatory sections that go before the references, while the Reproducibility Checklist is a separate form submitted during the review process. Let me check if either of these sections already exists in the paper.
```

(HumanEval/95)---a function that checks whether all dictionary keys are uniformly upper-case or lower-case strings.\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Ground-truth label:} \\\\texttt{pass}.\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Predicted label:} \\\\texttt{fail} (false negative).\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Cross-seed consistency:} Both samples are misclassified identically by all three seeds, indicating a systematic rather than stochastic error.\\n\\\\end{itemize}\\n\\nManual inspection reveals the root cause: \\\\texttt{check\\\\_dict\\\\_case} uses \\\\texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \\\\texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \\\\texttt{True} value, raising \\\\texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.\\nBecause \\\\texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \\\\texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \\\\texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines---even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.\\nThe model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \\\\texttt{fail}.\\nThis is not a model failure but a representation ceiling: the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \\\\texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.\\n\\nThese 2 irreducible errors establish a practical accuracy ceiling of $1 - 2/1190 = 99.83\\\\%$ for the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} condition on this dataset, suggesting that further improvements would require either supplementary trace signals or modifications to the input representation.\\n\", {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Discussion}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Why Exception Events Dominate}\\n\\nVerdict prediction is fundamentally a fault detection task: the model must determine whether execution deviated from expected behavior.\\nException events are the most explicit signal of such deviation---structured diagnostic moments that encode the nature and location of a fault.\\nUnlike loop compressions or assertion-point values, which require the model to integrate information across multiple trace elements, exception events provide a self-contained verdict cue.\\nThis explains why \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} matches or exceeds VOTA: adding non-exception trace components dilutes the model's attention on the diagnostic signal rather than providing complementary information.\\n\\nWe acknowledge that exception-based verdict prediction is, at its core, fault indicator presence detection: the model learns to map the presence or absence of a structured error message to a binary label, which is close to trivially solvable for any instruction-tuned LLM.\\nThe Tier~1 accuracy of ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% is therefore unsurprising in isolation.\\nThe contribution of this finding lies not in demonstrating that a strong signal yields high accuracy, but in two less obvious results: (a)~adding more trace information---loop compressions, assertion-point values---actively \\\\emph{hurts} performance (VOTA $<$ \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}), quantifying an information density dilution effect that has not been previously measured; and (b)~this dilution is confounded by tokenizer-driven trace truncation on DeepSeek: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing the majority of VOTA training samples to exceed the context window (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}); the verdict label is preserved, but the trace content is truncated, depriving the model of diagnostic information and contributing to the collapse of DeepSeek VOTA fail recall to 45.43\\\\% (Table~\\\\ref{tab:per_class}) while Qwen maintains 100\\\\%.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Simplicity as Relative Robustness}\\n\\nThe cross-model gap pattern (Table~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \\\\emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}\\nException events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity---a fixed-format error message---ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.\\nTwo of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\\\\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\\\\%) is attributable to LoRA initialization landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\\\\%), not to a failure of the exception signal itself---even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp.\\nThe VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency---the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}929, causing 91.7\\\\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating trace content while preserving the verdict label---and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \\\\citep{yang2024generalization}.\\nThe two factors cannot be disentangled in the current experimental design.\\nOur \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely by keeping inputs short (median 314 tokens), making its cross-model comparison clean and the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.\\nThe practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal; for smaller models, training stability (multi-seed selection, warmup schedules) is a secondary but addressable concern.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}\\n\\nThe DeepSeek \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.\\nFirst, epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in 2/3 seeds: seed 42 peaks at 99.83\\\\% after epoch~1 but settles \\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 4178 字符] ...\\n\\nnce termination) with the verdict label; when truncation is removed by extending the context window, the input distribution shifts out-of-distribution and the learned cue vanishes.\\nThis confirms that \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} accuracy reflects truncation pattern matching rather than genuine trace comprehension.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{VOTA is context-dependent.}\\nVOTA trained at \\\\texttt{max\\\\_length}=2{,}048 (where 91.7\\\\% of samples are truncated) achieves 84.45\\\\% when evaluated at \\\\texttt{max\\\\_length}=16{,}384, recovering performance by restoring the complete structured trace including the terminal ``Answer:'' prompt.\\nHowever, the reverse direction fails: evaluating at \\\\texttt{max\\\\_length}=2{,}048 yields only 16.0\\\\%, because content-aware truncation preferentially removes the terminal structured prompt that the model relies on for verdict extraction.\\nVOTA's accuracy is thus contingent on context length being sufficient to preserve its structured format---a fragility that scales with trace verbosity.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{Exception-only is inherently robust.}\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} maintains 99.33\\\\% accuracy regardless of evaluation context length.\\nBecause exception events are concise (median 314 tokens), no sample exceeds 2{,}048 tokens; varying \\\\texttt{max\\\\_length} between 2{,}048 and 16{,}384 produces identical inputs and therefore identical predictions.\\nThis robustness is not an engineered property but a natural consequence of signal brevity: there is no truncation to create distribution shift.\\n\\nThese results add an inference-time dimension to the signal quality argument.\\nException-based traces are not only the most accurate at training time (Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}) but also the most robust to deployment-time configuration changes---a practical advantage for systems where the evaluation context length may differ from the training setting.\\nThe pattern reinforces a consistent theme: \\\\emph{simpler, shorter signals are more robust because they have fewer failure modes.}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Limitations}\\n\\nOur study has several limitations.\\nFirst, all experiments use a single dataset (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); generalization to other languages, larger codebases, or different fault types remains untested.\\nSecond, we evaluate only two models at the 7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.\\nThird, DeepSeek \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\\\\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\\\\% ($\\\\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% for the outlier vs.\\\\ 100\\\\% for others).\\nQwen shows perfect seed robustness ($\\\\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$).\\nWhether training stabilization techniques (e.g., larger warmup ratio, LoRA rank adjustment) can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored.\\nOther conditions use a single seed.\\nFourth, the initial DeepSeek \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} run was invalidated by context overflow (the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2{,}048 tokens, truncating the verdict label); a stronger or weaker generator would yield a different class balance, potentially affecting task difficulty and the generalizability of our accuracy estimates.\\nFinally, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"6:All experiments use our trace--verdict dataset (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:dataset}) with $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\\\% confidence intervals unless stated otherwise.\\n38:Post-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\\n41:However, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\\\% (95\\\\% CI [80.75\\\\%, 85.24\\\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\\\\%).\\n51:Under deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's two errors yield 99.67\\\\% accuracy (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]), confirming that the result is robust to the effective sample size.\\n109:This two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.51}}"}

```text
- 10 solutions per problem = 5,910 trace-verdict pairs (67% pass, 33% fail)
- Test set: N=1,190 samples
- Training set has 2,858 unique after dedup, test set 607 unique
- DebugBench is only mentioned as future work in conclusion

Training details from appendix:
- Hardware: 1× RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell (94 GB VRAM)


- LoRA configuration with r=16, alpha=32, targeting all linear layers with 0.05 dropout
- Training for 3 epochs with batch size 32 (4 per device, 8 gradient accumulation steps)
- Learning rate of 2e-4 with linear decay, 10% warmup, and 0.01 weight decay
- Sequence length capped at 2048 tokens
- Multiple seeds (42, 123, 456) for robustness checks
- Using HuggingFace TRL's SFTTrainer framework
- Evaluation metrics include accuracy, per-class precision/recall/F1, and confidence intervals via Wilson and bootstrap methods
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}\\n\\\\usepackage[review]{acl}\\n% \\\\usepackage{acl}  % uncomment for camera-ready (remove [review])\\n\\\\usepackage{times,latexsym}\\n\\\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}\\n\\\\usepackage{graphicx}\\n\\\\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}\\n\\\\usepackage{booktabs}\\n\\\\usepackage{multirow}\\n\\\\usepackage{xcolor}\\n\\n\\\\title{Exception Events Are All You Need: \\\\\\\\Cross-Model Fault Detection for Verdict Prediction}\\n\\\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\\n\\n\\\\begin{document}\\n\\\\maketitle\\n\\n\\\\begin{abstract}\\nExecution traces are increasingly used to enhance code language models, but which trace components matter for downstream tasks remains poorly understood.\\nWe present the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for \\\\emph{verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---across two models (Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B).\\nOur key finding is that exception events alone achieve 99.83\\\\%/99.33\\\\% accuracy across models (cross-model gap = 0.50pp), while a multi-component representation (VOTA) degrades by 14.96pp on the weaker model.\\nWe decompose the gap between full traces and compressed representations into gradient dilution (26\\\\%) and information overload (74\\\\%), establishing that irrelevant trace content---not training signal quality---is the primary bottleneck.\\nComponent ablation reveals a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\\\\%.\\nThese results reframe verdict prediction as fault detection, where the minimal sufficient signal is a compact exception event that transfers across model families without requiring deep trace reasoning.\\n\\\\end{abstract}\\n\\n\\\\input{introduction}\\n\\\\input{method}\\n\\\\input{experiments}\\n\\\\input{discussion}\\n\\\\input{related_work}\\n\\\\input{conclusion}\\n\\n\\n\\\\\\\\section*{Ethics Statement}\\n\\nThis work uses publicly available programming problems from HumanEval \\\\\\\\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\\\\\\\citep{austin2021program}.\\nAll execution traces and pass/fail labels are generated through fully automated program execution; no human annotators or participants were involved.\\nThe dataset contains only source code and execution outputs with no personally identifiable information, offensive content, or privacy-sensitive data.\\nAll experiments were conducted on a single RTX PRO 6000 GPU, representing modest computational costs and environmental impact.\\nWe will publicly release our code, dataset, and trained LoRA adapters to support reproducibility and future Events Are All You Need: \\\\\\\\Cross-Model Fault Detection for Verdict Prediction}\\n\\\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\\n\\n\\\\begin{document}\\n\\\\maketitle\\n\\n\\\\begin{abstract}\\nExecution traces are increasingly used to enhance code language models, but which trace components matter for downstream tasks remains poorly understood.\\nWe present the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for \\\\emph{verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---across two models (Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B).\\nOur key finding is that exception events alone achieve 99.83\\\\%/99.33\\\\% accuracy across models (cross-model gap = 0.50pp), while a multi-component representation (VOTA) degrades by 14.96pp on the weaker model.\\nWe decompose the gap between full traces and compressed representations into gradient dilution (26\\\\%) and information overload (74\\\\%), establishing that irrelevant trace content---not training signal quality---is the primary bottleneck.\\nComponent ablation reveals a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\\\\%.\\nThese results reframe verdict prediction as fault detection, where the minimal sufficient signal is a compact exception event that transfers across model families without requiring deep trace reasoning.\\n\\\\end{abstract}\\n\\n\\\\input{introduction}\\n\\\\input{method}\\n\\\\input{experiments}\\n\\\\input{discussion}\\n\\\\input{related_work}\\n\\\\input{conclusion}\\n\\n\\\\section*{Ethics Statement}\\n\\nThis work uses publicly available programming problems from HumanEval \\\\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\\\citep{austin2021program}.\\nAll execution traces and pass/fail labels are generated through fully automated program execution; no human annotators or participants were involved.\\nThe dataset contains only source code and execution outputs with no personally identifiable information, offensive content, or privacy-sensitive data.\\nAll experiments were conducted on a single RTX PRO 6000 GPU, representing modest computational costs and
```
